I'll analyze the foraging mission carefully.

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score decreases by 1 for delivering the "good" item (one color half), increases by 1 for the "bad" item
- We need robots to: go to the correct source half, pick up the good item, return to nest
- The nest has WHITE floor, source has BLACK floor, middle is GRAY
- Nest-side wall is RED, one source half has BLUE walls, other has GREEN walls

**Key insight:** We need robots to go to the BLUE or GREEN half specifically. Since we don't know which decreases score, we need to try both. The robot should:
1. Navigate from nest (white) through gray to source (black)
2. Pick up item at one colored half (go toward BLUE or GREEN)
3. Return to nest (go toward RED wall)

**State design:**
- State 0: Explore/go toward source from nest area
- State 1: Go toward BLUE walls (target color)
- State 2: Go toward RED wall (return to nest)

Transitions: On WHITE floor → explore toward source; On BLACK floor → return; On BLACK floor with BLUE seen → collect; etc.

Let me design 14 machines covering different strategies:

**Reasoning:** Use 3-state machines: explore to find source, go toward BLUE (or GREEN) to get good item, go toward RED to return to nest. Trigger returns on BLACK floor (at source), trigger collection on seeing BLUE/GREEN, trigger return to nest on WHITE floor after collection.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I need to see the results from Round 2 to know which machines performed best. Since no scores were provided, I'll reason from first principles and diversify further.

**Key observations:**
- BLUE walls (color 3) and GREEN walls (color 2) are behind the source halves
- RED wall (color 1) is on the nest side
- WHITE=nest, BLACK=source, GRAY=middle
- Color codes: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

**Strategy:** The core loop is: go to source (toward BLACK floor), enter one specific half (toward BLUE or GREEN wall), return to nest (toward RED wall). We don't know which half gives the good item, so we try both. The robot needs to commit to one color to avoid picking up bad items.

**State machines:**
- 2-state: GoToColor(GREEN/BLUE) → on BLACK → GoToColor(RED); on WHITE → back
- 3-state: Explore → on BLACK go to specific color → on WHITE return via RED
- Include variants with BLUE vs GREEN targeting
- Add some with neighbor-based coordination

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
Since I'm not receiving score feedback, I need to reason more carefully about what should work and diversify my approach.

**Deep analysis:**
- Color IDs: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan
- RED wall = nest side (color 1), BLUE walls = one source half (color 3), GREEN walls = other source half (color 2)
- WHITE floor = nest, BLACK floor = source, GRAY = middle
- The robot should go toward one specific colored wall to collect that item, then return to RED wall

**Critical insight I may have missed:** When in "return" state going toward RED wall, the robot should already be carrying an item. The transition back to collecting should happen when on WHITE floor (delivered). The transition to return should happen on BLACK floor (picked up item).

**Better loop:** 
- State 0: GoToColor(GREEN or BLUE) - heading to source half
- On BLACK floor → State 1: GoToColor(RED) - return to nest  
- On WHITE floor → State 0: back to collecting

This 2-state machine is the cleanest. I'll also try 3-state variants with an exploration intermediate state.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
Since I'm still not receiving score feedback, I need to think more carefully about potential issues with my designs.

**Critical re-examination:**

The simplest correct foraging loop should be:
- GoToColor(GREEN wall=2 OR BLUE wall=3) → when BLACK floor detected → GoToColor(RED wall=1) → when WHITE floor detected → repeat

The 2-state machine with single transitions is the purest form. But maybe I'm missing something about how items work - the robot gets an item just by ENTERING the half, so we don't need to "wait" there.

**Potential problem:** My robots may be oscillating near the source without penetrating deep enough, or getting confused between GRAY and transitions. I should try:
1. Higher probabilities on floor transitions (0.99)
2. Different approach: use GoAwayColor(RED) to reach source instead
3. Use GRAY floor detection to trigger intermediate behaviors
4. Try GoAwayColor variants - go away from RED means go toward source

**New ideas:**
- GoAwayColor(RED) to reach source (avoids needing to distinguish BLUE/GREEN)
- Then GoToColor(RED) to return
- Transition on BLACK (at source) and WHITE (at nest)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
Since I'm still not receiving score feedback, I need to fundamentally reconsider my approach.

**Deep rethink:**

The score is MINIMISED. One item type decreases score by 1 (good), the other increases by 1 (bad). We don't know which is which from the problem statement alone - it could be either BLUE or GREEN half.

**What if the optimal strategy is to deliver BOTH types but avoid one?** No - we need to consistently go to one half only.

**Critical issue I may have overlooked:** Maybe my robots aren't actually completing trips because:
1. GoToColor needs the colored object to be VISIBLE - walls may only be visible when close
2. From the nest (WHITE), the source walls (BLUE/GREEN) may not be visible at all
3. GoAwayColor(RED) may be better for leaving nest since RED wall is always visible from nest side

**Better design principle:**
- From nest: GoAwayColor(RED) naturally pushes toward source
- At source (BLACK): GoToColor(GREEN or BLUE) to commit to one half  
- After collecting (still BLACK): GoToColor(RED) to return
- Need a way to distinguish "going to source" from "returning" - use LED colors as memory proxy, or use GRAY floor as intermediate

**Simplest reliable loop:**
- State 0 (at nest, WHITE): GoAwayColor(RED) → BLACK detected → State 1
- State 1 (at source, BLACK): GoToColor(RED) → WHITE detected → State 0

This avoids needing to see source walls and always delivers whatever item was picked up. But this collects random items (50/50 good/bad) = score ~0. We need to be selective.

**Actually:** The robot enters whichever half it happens to be in. To score negatively we need to ONLY enter the good half. The trick is to approach from the correct side using BLUE or GREEN wall guidance.

Let me try a 3-state machine where:
- State 0: GoAwayColor(RED) until GRAY, then steer toward specific color
- State 1: GoToColor(BLUE or GREEN) until BLACK, collect item
- State 2: GoToColor(RED) until WHITE, deliver

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
Since I'm still not receiving score feedback, I need to radically rethink my approach.

**Fundamental reassessment:**

I've been assuming the same basic structure for 6 rounds. Let me think about what might actually be happening:

1. The score might be close to 0 (random) because robots are entering both halves equally
2. My GoToColor approaches may not be working well because walls may not be visible from far away
3. The key problem: how does a robot ONLY enter the good half?

**What if I try completely different approaches:**

**Approach A - Exploit floor colors only:** Just do the simple loop GoAwayRed→BLACK→GoToRed→WHITE. This gives ~0 score (random half). Not great but reliable.

**Approach B - Use neighbor-based coordination:** Robots follow each other to concentrate at one source half.

**Approach C - Pure exploration with floor triggers:** Random walk, collect whatever, return on WHITE. Score ~0.

**Approach D - Try GoToColor with YELLOW (4), MAGENTA (5), CYAN (6):** These are LED colors that robots themselves emit! If robots in state "carrying good item" emit a specific LED color, other robots can follow them to the good half. This is stigmergy!

**The LED trick:** 
- Robots going to GREEN half emit YELLOW LEDs
- Robots returning (carrying item) emit MAGENTA LEDs  
- Other robots can follow YELLOW-lit robots toward the source
- C=7 (perceives color) can detect other robots' LEDs!

This is the key insight I've been missing - use robot LEDs as communication signals!

**Design:**
- State 0: GoToColor(GREEN wall=2), LED=YELLOW → on BLACK → State 1
- State 1: GoToColor(RED wall=1), LED=MAGENTA → on WHITE → State 0
- Other robots see YELLOW → follow toward source; see MAGENTA → avoid (or go to nest)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
Since I'm receiving no score feedback, I need to think about this more carefully and try radically different approaches.

**Complete rethink:**

The score is minimized. One half gives -1, the other +1. Without knowing which is which, maybe the optimizer has been finding this out through simulation. Let me think about what structures would robustly score negative:

**Key insight I may be missing:** The problem says "an item from one half decreases the score by 1" - this is fixed per run. So consistently going to one specific half (say GREEN=color 2) will either always decrease or always increase score. The optimizer should have discovered which one works by now through the 600+ simulations.

**What if I try: robots that ONLY go to one half and return?**

The most minimal correct behavior:
- Go toward GREEN walls (clr=2) until BLACK floor → return toward RED (clr=1) until WHITE floor → repeat
- Same with BLUE walls (clr=3)

**Critical new idea:** What if robots should go AWAY from the bad color? If GREEN is bad, going away from GREEN naturally takes you to BLUE half. GoAwayColor(GREEN) while approaching source.

**Also trying:** Use GRAY floor as a "commit" point - when on GRAY, steer toward target color; when on BLACK, immediately return.

Let me try the most streamlined versions plus some with GoAwayColor for source selection:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
Since I'm still receiving no score feedback, I need to make my most carefully reasoned attempt yet with only 2 rounds remaining.

**Final deep analysis:**

The core issue: I don't know which half (GREEN=2 or BLUE=3) is the good one. Both could be tested. But the optimizer should be finding which works better across simulations.

**What I know for certain about the mechanics:**
- Robot enters BLACK floor → gets item of that half's type (replaces current item)
- Robot enters WHITE floor with item → delivers it, score changes
- The loop: source → nest → source → nest is what we want to maximize throughput of

**The simplest possible correct design** (2 states, 1 transition each):
- S0: GoToColor(X) [heading to source half X] → on BLACK → S1
- S1: GoToColor(RED=1) [heading to nest] → on WHITE → S0

This is clean. The question is just which X (2 or 3) scores negative.

**New hypothesis:** Maybe the issue is that GoToColor with wall colors doesn't work well because the camera has limited range and the walls may not always be visible. The robots might be spending time lost.

**Alternative: use floor colors only with GoAwayColor:**
- GoAwayColor(RED=1) takes you from nest toward source
- GoToColor(RED=1) takes you from source toward nest
- But this doesn't select a specific half!

**Best bet:** Submit the cleanest versions of both BLUE and GREEN targeting, plus some with GRAY floor as intermediate trigger, and some pure floor-based navigation.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
This is the final round. I need to make my best possible submission.

**Final reasoning:**

After 9 rounds of exploration without feedback, I've been systematically trying both BLUE (clr=3) and GREEN (clr=2) targeting with GoToColor/GoAwayColor combinations. The core logic is sound - the question is which wall color leads to the score-decreasing item.

**What I know works structurally:**
- 2-state: GoToColor(X) → BLACK → GoToColor(RED=1) → WHITE → repeat
- 3-state: adds exploration/intermediate state
- The transition probabilities at 0.99 are cleanest

**For this final round, I'll:**
1. Keep the best structural variants (elitism - all my core designs)
2. Cover BOTH color options (GREEN=2 and BLUE=3) equally since I don't know which is good
3. Include the GoAwayColor(RED) approach as it reliably moves away from nest
4. Try one novel idea: use GRAY floor transition as an intermediate trigger to steer toward specific color BEFORE reaching BLACK (so robot commits to correct half earlier)
5. Include variants with slightly different probability tuning

The cleanest 2-state machines with single transitions are my most reliable bets - minimal complexity, clear behavior.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
